Not so much a plan, but I kept thinking my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up. Even after church, I don’t really stay and talk like I used to. It all feels kind of far away from me.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're not thinking about your daughter leaving, do you still feel depressed?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 57 / general_or_operations_manager / less_than_9th\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. This one after she left has just been the worst.\n\nDoctor: During those earlier low stretches before your daughter left, did you lose interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. Things I normally liked just didn’t pull me in. I used to mess with my tomato plants, work on sanding wood at my bench, watch the Falcons, stay after church and talk with folks… but during those stretches, I’d just look at all that like it belonged to somebody else. Didn’t feel like doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down like this, on and off or most days, for two years or longer?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s been two years. Looking back, it’s more like these low stretches have been coming around for about a year. This one after my daughter left is the one that’s really hit me the hardest.\n\nDoctor: During one of those earlier low stretches before your daughter left, were you also having thoughts that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yes… I was having thoughts like that. Not so much a plan, but I kept thinking my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up. It scared me enough that I told my supervisor, and that’s part of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Since your daughter left, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. Since she left, it’s like the things I used to enjoy don’t mean much. I’ll see my tomato plants, my wood bench, even the Falcons game on, and I just… don’t feel like messing with any of it. Even after church, I don’t really stay and talk like I used to. It all feels kind of far away from me.\n\nDoctor: When you're not thinking about your daughter leaving, do you still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, she’s on my mind a lot, so it’s hard to separate it out. But even when I’m doing something else, that heavy feeling is still kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Since your daughter left, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been keeping track of my sleep much. And at home… I’ve just been letting bills and regular responsibilities stack up instead of taking care of them like I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you been feeling down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say most of the day every day. It comes on pretty heavy when I start thinking about her leaving, and that’s been happening a lot… but I haven’t really kept track of whether it’s all day.\n\nDoctor: Before your daughter left, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I think I’ve had lower stretches off and on for about a year now. But this one, after my daughter left, has definitely hit me the hardest.\n\nDoctor: Before your daughter left, was there ever a period when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I think so, yeah. Looking back, I’ve had these low stretches coming and going for about a year. I can’t pin down every exact week, but there were times before my daughter left where it felt like I was down nearly every day for a couple weeks. This one after she left has just been the worst.\n\nDoctor: During those earlier low stretches before your daughter left, did you lose interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. Things I normally liked just didn’t pull me in. I used to mess with my tomato plants, work on sanding wood at my bench, watch the Falcons, stay after church and talk with folks… but during those stretches, I’d just look at all that like it belonged to somebody else. Didn’t feel like doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down like this, on and off or most days, for two years or longer?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s been two years. Looking back, it’s more like these low stretches have been coming around for about a year. This one after my daughter left is the one that’s really hit me the hardest.\n\nDoctor: During one of those earlier low stretches before your daughter left, were you also having thoughts that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yes… I was having thoughts like that. Not so much a plan, but I kept thinking my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up. It scared me enough that I told my supervisor, and that’s part of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Since your daughter left, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. Since she left, it’s like the things I used to enjoy don’t mean much. I’ll see my tomato plants, my wood bench, even the Falcons game on, and I just… don’t feel like messing with any of it. Even after church, I don’t really stay and talk like I used to. It all feels kind of far away from me.\n\nDoctor: When you're not thinking about your daughter leaving, do you still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, she’s on my mind a lot, so it’s hard to separate it out. But even when I’m doing something else, that heavy feeling is still kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Since your daughter left, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been keeping track of my sleep much. Some nights feel off, but I can’t say for sure it’s been a big trouble sleeping thing.\n\nDoctor: Since your daughter left, have you noticed your appetite has gone down?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. Most days I kind of have to make myself eat a few bites. Stuff I usually like, whether it’s food at the warehouse or something at home, I just leave it sitting there, and then end up throwing it out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I came in because I’ve been having thoughts that my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up. That scared me… scared me enough that I told my supervisor, and they said I should get help.\n\nDoctor: Are you thinking about killing yourself right now?\n\nPatient: Right now, I’m having those thoughts like… my family might be better off if I didn’t wake up. I don’t want to feel that way, and it scared me enough that I told my supervisor.\n\nDoctor: Do you want to kill yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t want to kill myself, no. It’s more that I keep having this thought that my family might be better off if I didn’t wake up. And that scared me enough to tell my supervisor.\n\nDoctor: Did something stressful happen in your life before these thoughts started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my daughter left Georgia for the military about two weeks ago, and since then this whole change in me has been going on.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings seem mostly about your daughter leaving for the military?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly. I keep going back over her leaving and I start telling myself I must’ve failed her somehow… like if I’d been a better father, I would’ve done something different.\n\nDoctor: When you’re not thinking about your daughter leaving, do you still feel down?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, most of it seems tied to her leaving. If I’m not thinking about that, I don’t know that I feel as bad… but it’s hard to separate it right now.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, has it been harder for you to function at work or at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. At work I’ve been messing up more than usual, enough that my supervisor found me sitting in my truck before dawn and ended up getting occupational health involved. And at home… I’ve just been letting bills and regular responsibilities stack up instead of taking care of them like I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Since this started, have you been feeling down most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say most of the day every day. It comes on pretty heavy when I start thinking about her leaving, and that’s been happening a lot… but I haven’t really kept track of whether it’s all day.\n\nDoctor: Before your daughter left, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed like this?\n\nPatient: Yeah… looking back, I think I’ve had lower stretches off and on for about a year now. But this one, after my daughter left, has definitely hit me the hardest.\n\nDoctor: Before your daughter left, was there ever a period when you felt depressed nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I think so, yeah. Looking back, I’ve had these low stretches coming and going for about a year. I can’t pin down every exact week, but there were times before my daughter left where it felt like I was down nearly every day for a couple weeks. This one after she left has just been the worst.\n\nDoctor: During those earlier low stretches before your daughter left, did you lose interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. Things I normally liked just didn’t pull me in. I used to mess with my tomato plants, work on sanding wood at my bench, watch the Falcons, stay after church and talk with folks… but during those stretches, I’d just look at all that like it belonged to somebody else. Didn’t feel like doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down like this, on and off or most days, for two years or longer?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it’s been two years. Looking back, it’s more like these low stretches have been coming around for about a year. This one after my daughter left is the one that’s really hit me the hardest.\n\nDoctor: During one of those earlier low stretches before your daughter left, were you also having thoughts that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yes… I was having thoughts like that. Not so much a plan, but I kept thinking my family might be better off if I just didn’t wake up. It scared me enough that I told my supervisor, and that’s part of why I came in.\n\nDoctor: Since your daughter left, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. Since she left, it’s like the things I used to enjoy don’t mean much. I’ll see my tomato plants, my wood bench, even the Falcons game on, and I just… don’t feel like messing with any of it. Even after church, I don’t really stay and talk like I used to. It all feels kind of far away from me.\n\nDoctor: When you're not thinking about your daughter leaving, do you still feel depressed?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, she’s on my mind a lot, so it’s hard to separate it out. But even when I’m doing something else, that heavy feeling is still kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Since your daughter left, have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been keeping track of my sleep much. Some nights feel off, but I can’t say for sure it’s been a big trouble sleeping thing.\n\nDoctor: Since your daughter left, have you noticed your appetite has gone down?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has. Most days I kind of have to make myself eat a few bites. Stuff I usually like, whether it’s food at the warehouse or something at home, I just leave it sitting there, and then end up throwing it out.\n\nDoctor: Before your daughter left, did you ever have a period of at least two weeks when you felt depressed nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… before she left, I’d had low periods that would come and go for maybe about a year. I’m not sure I counted the days exactly, but there were stretches where I felt down most days. This one since she left has been the hardest, though.\n\nDoctor: Since your daughter left, have you had trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Yeah. At work, especially at the distribution center, I’ll be looking right at the schedule or the routing sheet and it’s like my mind won’t settle on it. Even small staffing decisions feel harder than they should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
